description: "How the ZX Spectrum Next's TurboSound Next puts three AY chips behind the 128K's two ports: the select values that choose a chip and set its pan, what old software sees with TurboSound off, how ABC, ACB and mono spread each chip's three channels over the two sides, panning each chip, how much of the mixer nine channels take, what a reset clears, what programs use it for, and how Klive emulates it."

import { Callout } from 'nextra/components'

# TurboSound: Three AY Chips Behind Two Ports

Two ports, three chips. In most of engineering, that sentence is the opening line of a bug report. On the
Next, well, it is a feature. Load a 128K game that has never heard of the Next, and its tune plays on the
first AY chip exactly as it did on the 128K. Load a Next tracker, and you hear nine channels at once, each chip
parked at its own spot between your left and right ear. So how can three chips share two ports without the
old software ever noticing that it has company?

Have you ever written music for one AY chip? Then you know how tight three voices are. You get a melody, a
bass, and one more voice for everything else, and the drums have to elbow their way in between whenever the
other three take a breath. Three chips give you nine. That is enough for full chords, a drum part of its own,
and sound effects that no longer have to mug the melody for a channel. And because you can place each chip
left, right or in the middle, you can spread a tune across the stereo field like a mixing desk, minus the
coffee stains.

Everything here builds on [One AY Chip: Tones, Noise and Envelopes](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/psg/), which takes a
single chip apart, with its registers and ports `$FFFD` and `$BFFD`. I will not repeat any of
that here; we only look at what changes when there are three of them. If stereo and panning are new to you,
the background chapter's section
[Two speakers](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/computer-sound/#two-speakers-stereo-and-space) explains
them in a few minutes. Go on, I will wait.

## The road ahead

Uhm, I have to confess something: the whole of TurboSound rests on one small idea. Some values a program can
write to port `$FFFD` can never be a register number, so the Next is free to give them a new meaning, "talk to
this chip from now on". We start there, because everything else follows from it, and then play one note to see
the idea at work. Next comes the switch that turns TurboSound on and off, and the curious way the chosen chip
freezes while it is off. Then we look at where each chip's three channels land between your two ears, in ABC,
ACB or mono, and how you can push a whole chip to one side. After that, a look at how much of the mixer nine
channels at full volume really take, and what a reset clears. We end with what programs do with all this, and
how Klive does the same.

<Callout type="info">
Levels are in the **units of the Next's mixer**: one AY channel at full volume adds 255. The chips are in
YM2149 mode, and every capture plays channel B, or all three channels, at full volume. The waveforms are
captured from Klive's emulated Next at 48,000 samples a second, from programs checked to do what the text
says; they show Klive's mix, where the AY chips count up from 0 as they do on the hardware.
</Callout>

## Three chips, two ports

The Next has three AY chips, numbered **0, 1 and 2**. All three listen to the 128K's two ports, **port
`$FFFD`** (select a register) and **port `$BFFD`** (write it). But only one of them is allowed to answer: the
**selected chip**. Whatever you write, and whatever you read, goes to that chip, while the other two politely
look the other way.

<figure>
{/* fig:flow */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 360" role="img" aria-label="A flow diagram. A value written to port $FFFD is tested. If bits 7 to 5 are 000, it selects a register in the selected chip. If bit 7 is 1 and bits 4 to 2 are 111, and TurboSound is on, it selects chip 0, 1 or 2 and sets that chip's pan. Anything else is ignored. Separately, port $BFFD writes the selected register of the selected chip. Of the three chips, the selected one is marked as the one the ports reach." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

{/* /fig:flow */}
<figcaption>What happens to a value you write to port $FFFD. With bits 7–5 clear, it selects a register in the selected chip; with bit 7 set and bits 4–2 all 1, it selects a chip and sets its pan, as long as TurboSound is on; anything else is ignored. Port $BFFD always writes the selected chip.</figcaption>
</figure>

Here is the trick, and I think it is a lovely one. A register number is 0 to 31, so when a 128K program
selects a register, **bits 7–5 are always 0**. It has no reason to put anything else there. Those upper values
are an empty room in an old house, and TurboSound simply moves in and gives some of them a meaning of their own:

<figure>
{/* fig:fffd */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 220" role="img" aria-label="Two strips of eight bits, bit 7 on the left. A register select: bits 7, 6 and 5 are 0, and bits 4 to 0 hold the register number, 0 to 31. A chip select: bit 7 is 1, bit 6 is the left pan, bit 5 the right pan, bits 4 to 2 are 1, 1, 1, and bits 1 to 0 choose the chip: 11 chip 0, 10 chip 1, 01 chip 2." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

  <rect x="477" y="112" width="71" height="52" rx="5" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1.4" />
  <text x="512.5" y="143" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">1</text>
  <rect x="552" y="112" width="146" height="52" rx="5" fill="#e8912d" fillOpacity="0.16" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="2" />

  <text x="625" y="152" textAnchor="middle" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">10 = 1, 01 = 2</text>
  <text x="100" y="206" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">$FF, $FE and $FD select chips 0, 1 and 2 on both sides</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:fffd */}
<figcaption>Port $FFFD written two ways, bit 7 on the left. Top: a register select, bits 7–5 clear and the register number in bits 4–0. Bottom: a chip select, bit 7 set, the pan in bits 6–5, bits 4–2 all 1, and the chip in bits 1–0.</figcaption>
</figure>

- **Bit 7** is 1: "this is not a register number".
- **Bits 4–2** are `111`: "and I really mean it: I am choosing a chip".
- **Bits 1–0** pick the chip: `11` for chip 0, `10` for chip 1, `01` for chip 2. (`00` picks chip 0 as well.)
- **Bits 6–5** set that chip's **pan**, which you meet properly a little later: bit 6 lets the chip into the
  left side, bit 5 into the right.

With both pan bits set, you get three values that are easy to remember: `$FF` for chip 0, `$FE` for chip 1 and
`$FD` for chip 2. And what if you write a value with bit 7 set but some other pattern in bits 4–2, or one with
bit 7 clear but bits 6–5 not both 0? Nothing at all. The Next simply shrugs.

Hey, notice how neat this is. An old program cannot select a chip by accident, because it never writes such a
value. A new program needs no new port, because the old one had room to spare all along. It is the kind of
design I wish I had come up with myself.

Well, there is one small catch: none of this works until TurboSound is switched on. You will see what that
means in a moment.

## One note, five writes

Let's play a note on chip 1's channel A. It takes exactly one write more than it would on a 128K: you select
the chip first, and then carry on as you always did.

<figure>
{/* fig:sequence */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 270" role="img" aria-label="Five writes from left to right on a time line, each with the chip it reaches. First, $FE to port $FFFD selects chip 1 and reaches no register. Then $00 to port $FFFD selects register 0 of chip 1; $F9 to port $BFFD writes chip 1's R0; $08 to port $FFFD selects chip 1's R8; $0F to port $BFFD writes it. Rows for chips 0 and 2 receive nothing." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

    </marker>

  <text x="0" y="215" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">chip 2</text>
  <line x1="70" y1="209" x2="700" y2="209" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="1" strokeDasharray="2 3" strokeOpacity="0.4" />
  <text x="70" y="256" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">the chip select chooses the chip; it reaches no register</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:sequence */}
<figcaption>Five writes on a time line, and the chip each one reaches. The chip select reaches no register at all; the register select and the data that follow go to chip 1, the selected chip, and so does every write after them until you select another chip.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. Write `$FE` to port `$FFFD`: chip 1 is now the selected chip (and plays on both sides).
2. Write `0` to port `$FFFD`: you have selected register 0 of chip 1.
3. Write the period's low byte to port `$BFFD`: it lands in chip 1's R0.
4. Write `8` to port `$FFFD`, then the volume to port `$BFFD`: chip 1's R8, and the note sounds.

That's all. One thing I want you to remember from this little exercise, though: each chip keeps its own
selected register, like three clerks who each keep their own place in the ledger. So when you switch from one
chip to another, select the register again, and do not count on the last one you used. The chip itself stays
selected until your next chip select, which is why a music player usually visits each chip once per frame and
writes all of its registers in one go.

Reading works the same way: port `$FFFD` gives you the selected register of the selected chip. And if your
program has lost track of whom it is talking to (it happens to the best of us), port `$BFF5` will tell it, as
[AY or YM](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/psg/#ay-or-ym) in the previous chapter
explains. Its bits 7–6 read `11`, `10` or `01`, the same codes the select uses, and bits 4–0 hold the selected
register.

## TurboSound on, TurboSound off

TurboSound has a switch: bit 1 of the **Peripheral 3 Setting register, NextReg `$08`**. It is off after
power-on, and then your Next does a very convincing impression of a 128K:

- **Only the selected chip sounds.** The other two stay silent, whatever their registers hold.
- **The selection is frozen.** The Next ignores every chip select, so the selected chip cannot change. After
  power-on that is chip 0, which is why every 128K program plays on chip 0 and never suspects a thing.

"Frozen" means exactly what it says: the selection stays wherever it was when the music stopped, like a game of
musical chairs. Switch TurboSound on, select chip 2, switch TurboSound off again, and chip 2 stays selected. All
your writes go to it, and it is the only chip you hear. Nothing is lost meanwhile. All three chips keep their
registers, and when you switch TurboSound back on, the other two come back with whatever they held, as if they
had never left the room.

Let me show you the same little program three ways. It selects each chip in turn and gives it one note on
channel B: C on chip 0, E on chip 1, G on chip 2.

<figure>
{/* fig:onoff */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 354" role="img" aria-label="Three captures of the left side, ten milliseconds each, from 0 to 765 units. TurboSound on: three square waves added, C, E and G, a stepped wave reaching 765. TurboSound off from the start: a single square wave, G, 255 units. TurboSound switched off after the chord was set up: again a single square wave, G, now chip 2's." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>
  <line x1="140" y1="90" x2="696" y2="90" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.3" />

<figcaption>Three captures of the same program, 10 ms each. TurboSound on: all three notes. TurboSound off from the start: the selects are ignored, all three setups land in chip 0, and only the last note, G, survives. TurboSound switched off after the chord was set up, with chip 2 selected last: you hear chip 2's G alone.</figcaption>
</figure>

Look at the middle one closely. I mean it: this is what your shiny TurboSound program sounds like on a Next
where nobody switched TurboSound on. Its selects are ignored, every write lands in chip 0, and the three chips'
parts overwrite each other there until only the last one is left standing. So here is my advice, and it is the
most important sentence in this section: if your program wants three chips, it must switch TurboSound on
itself. Never assume someone else already did.

## Where the channels go

Each chip has three channels, but you have only two ears. Every chip has to decide where its A, B and C go,
and the Next gives you three ways to settle the argument. It adds the channels up as it spreads them:

<figure>
{/* fig:spread */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 290" role="img" aria-label="Three panels: ABC, ACB and mono. In each, channels A, B and C are drawn as small boxes placed between a left and a right label according to where they are heard, with the sum each side adds. ABC: A at the left, B in the middle, C at the right; left A plus B, right C plus B, at most 510 each. ACB: A at the left, C in the middle, B at the right; left A plus C, right C plus B. Mono: A, B and C all in the middle; both sides A plus B plus C, at most 765." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

  <text x="484" y="60" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">left</text>
  <text x="688" y="60" textAnchor="end" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">right</text>
  <line x1="488" y1="72" x2="684" y2="72" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.4" />
  <rect x="565" y="85" width="42" height="34" rx="5" fill="#e8912d" fillOpacity="0.2" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="2" />
  <text x="586" y="108" textAnchor="middle" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="16.5" fontWeight="bold">A</text>
  <rect x="565" y="127" width="42" height="34" rx="5" fill="#e8912d" fillOpacity="0.2" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="2" />
  <text x="586" y="150" textAnchor="middle" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="16.5" fontWeight="bold">B</text>
  <rect x="565" y="169" width="42" height="34" rx="5" fill="#e8912d" fillOpacity="0.2" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="2" />
  <text x="586" y="192" textAnchor="middle" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="16.5" fontWeight="bold">C</text>
  <text x="586" y="232" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15" fontFamily="monospace">L = A + B + C</text>
  <text x="586" y="254" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15" fontFamily="monospace">R = A + B + C</text>
  <text x="586" y="276" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">at most 765 a side</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:spread */}
<figcaption>Where one chip's three channels go, and what each side adds. ABC: A on the left, C on the right, B on both. ACB: A on the left, B on the right, C on both. Mono: all three on both sides.</figcaption>
</figure>

- **ABC** (what you get after power-on): left = **A + B**, right = **C + B**. A sits on the left, C on the
  right, and B, which both sides hear equally, in the middle.
- **ACB**: left = **A + C**, right = **C + B**. A on the left, B on the right, and now **C is in the middle**.
- **Mono**: both sides = **A + B + C**. Everybody in the middle, shoulder to shoulder.

I always remember them like this: read the name from left to right, and the middle letter is the channel in
the middle. In ABC that is B; in ACB, C. Simple. And the middle seat is the best one in the house: it is where a
musician puts the part that must not lean to either side, usually the bass or the melody.

Bit 5 of NextReg `$08` chooses ACB (1) or ABC (0), for all three chips at once. Bits 7–5 of the **Peripheral 4
Setting register, NextReg `$09`**, put chips 2, 1 and 0 in mono, each on its own, and for a chip in mono, mono
wins over ACB. What does that mean for the levels? With one chip at full volume:

- In ABC or ACB, each side adds two channels: at most 2 × 255 = **510**.
- In mono, each side adds all three: at most 3 × 255 = **765**, on both sides.

Now, have you wondered why you would want a choice at all? It comes down to how a tune is mixed. Its composer
had one layout in mind, and put the part that belongs in the middle on the middle channel. Play it with the
other layout, and that part slides off to one side while a side part wanders into the middle. On headphones you
hear it at once: a balanced tune turns lopsided, like a band whose drummer set up in the car park. Spectrum
music was not all written for the same layout, so the Next offers both. And mono? That one is for the tunes
written for the 128K's single speaker.

## Panning a whole chip

ABC, ACB and mono place the channels inside a chip. **Panning** then places the whole chip. Every chip has a
2-bit **pan**, and you set it with bits 6–5 of the very value that selects the chip:

- `11`: both sides (what every chip has after a reset).
- `10`: left only.
- `01`: right only.
- `00`: muted, the chip's equivalent of being sent to its room.

The pan works on the chip's own mix, after ABC, ACB or mono. With pan `10`, the chip's left side goes to the
mixer's left, and its right side is dropped. Let's try it with three chips, each playing one note on
channel B (the middle channel in ABC), with chip 0 panned left, chip 1 to both sides and chip 2 right:

<figcaption>The left and right sides of one capture, 10 ms each. Chip 0's C plays only on the left, chip 2's G only on the right, and chip 1's E on both: so the left side is C and E added, and the right side E and G.</figcaption>
</figure>

Now let me warn you about one thing, because it is very easy to get wrong: **every chip select sets the pan
again**. Say you panned chip 1 to the left. Later you select it with `$FE`, out of
sheer habit, and congratulations: you have just dragged it back to the middle. From then on, you must select it
with `$DE` (`%1101 1110`: bit 6 set, bit 5 clear). Oh, and a pan you write while TurboSound is off goes nowhere.
The Next ignores it together with the select it came in.

## Nine channels in the mixer

The mixer adds the three chips' sides into one AY input per side. How much of its range that takes depends on
the layout you chose:

<figure>
{/* fig:budget */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 290" role="img" aria-label="Three bars drawn to scale on the mixer's range of 0 to 8,191, with a dashed line at 4,095, the audio jack's limit. Each bar starts with the silent level of 1,536. Three chips in stereo at full volume add 1,530, reaching 3,066, under the line. Three chips in mono add 2,295, reaching 3,831, still under it. With the beeper's EAR on top, 512 more, the sum reaches 4,343, past the line." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>
  <text x="0" y="24" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">three chips in stereo, every channel at full volume</text>
  <rect x="0" y="34" width="130.9" height="30" fill="currentColor" fillOpacity="0.15" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.4" />
  <text x="65.4" y="55" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">silence</text>
  <rect x="130.9" y="34" width="130.4" height="30" fill="#e8912d" fillOpacity="0.75" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="1" />
  <text x="196.1" y="55" textAnchor="middle" fill="#ffffff" fontSize="13.5" fontWeight="bold">AY 1,530</text>
  <text x="700" y="55" textAnchor="end" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">sum 3,066</text>
  <text x="0" y="90" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">three chips in mono, every channel at full volume</text>

  <text x="700" y="187" textAnchor="end" fill="#e5484d" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">sum 4,343</text>
  <line x1="349" y1="12" x2="349" y2="226" stroke="#e5484d" strokeWidth="2.2" strokeDasharray="6 4" />
  <text x="355" y="224" fill="#e5484d" fontSize="13.5">4,095: the jack's limit</text>
  <line x1="0" y1="246" x2="700" y2="246" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <line x1="0" y1="241" x2="0" y2="251" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <text x="0" y="270" textAnchor="start" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">0</text>
  <line x1="130.9" y1="241" x2="130.9" y2="251" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <text x="130.9" y="270" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">1,536</text>
  <line x1="349" y1="241" x2="349" y2="251" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <text x="349" y="270" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">4,095</text>
  <line x1="698" y1="241" x2="698" y2="251" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <text x="698" y="270" textAnchor="end" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">8,191</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:budget */}
<figcaption>The three chips' largest sum per side, drawn to scale on the mixer's range, on top of the silent level of 1,536. In stereo, nine channels at full volume add 1,530 and stay well under the audio jack's limit of 4,095; three chips in mono add 2,295, which still fits, but not with the beeper on top.</figcaption>
</figure>

- **Stereo** (ABC or ACB), every channel of every chip at full volume: 3 × 510 = **1,530** per side.
- **Mono** on all three chips: 3 × 765 = **2,295** per side, the most the AY input can ever carry.

The audio jack plays sums up to 4,095, and a silent Next already sits at 1,536 (the mixer chapter's
[Headroom](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/#headroom) explains why), so you have
2,559 left for everything else. Nine channels in stereo fit, with room to spare for the beeper. Three chips in
mono fit on their own too. But put the beeper's 512 on top, and the sum passes 4,095: the jack wraps around, and
you hear a crackle that no musician ordered.

Should you worry about it? Honestly, hardly ever. Channels are seldom all at full volume, and square waves at
different pitches are seldom all high at the same moment. Here is what nine channels at full volume look like,
three chords on three chips:

<figcaption>Nine channels at full volume, captured, 20 ms of each side: chip 0 plays C, E and G, chip 1 F, A and C, chip 2 G, B and D an octave higher, all in ABC. Each side touches 1,530 only at the rare moments when all its channels are high together, and spends most of its time far below.</figcaption>
</figure>

See those rare peaks that touch the top? That is all nine square waves happening to be high at the same
instant. It does happen, but only for a moment, and then the waves drift apart again.

## When the chips are reset

A reset clears more than the chips' registers. Every chip's registers go back to their reset values (all 0,
R7 `$FF`), so all three fall silent; **chip 0 is selected**, and register 0 in it; and every **pan goes back to
`11`**, both sides. In short, the Next tidies up the room and puts everyone back in the middle.

A soft reset does not touch the NextRegs, though. TurboSound's switch, ACB and the mono bits keep whatever they
held, and only a hard reset clears them. This one is easy to miss, so let me say it plainly: if your program
relies on ABC, set NextReg `$08` yourself rather than trusting that a reset did it for you.

There is one more way to reset the chips. Set **NextReg `$06` bits 1–0 to `11`**, and they stay in reset for as
long as the mode stays `11`. They are silent, their registers are cleared, chip 0 is selected, every pan is back
to both sides, and every write you make is ignored, however hard you insist. Choose YM or AY again, and they
start afresh from there.

## What programs do with nine channels

If you take only one idea about TurboSound into your own game, take this one: **keep music and effects
apart**. Play the music on chips 0 and 1 and the sound effects on chip 2, and an explosion never cuts a note
short. On a 128K, every effect had to steal a channel from the tune, and believe me, the tune always noticed.

Trackers for the Next write for all three chips at once: chords, a bass and drums of their own, and echo effects
made by repeating a part quietly on another chip. Panning adds a sense of space. Put a chip playing a pad on the
left and one playing an arpeggio on the right, keep the bass and the melody in the middle on channel B in ABC,
and suddenly a little 8-bit machine sounds like a room full of musicians.

And sometimes, well, the best thing to do with TurboSound is nothing at all. Leave it off, pick ABC or ACB to
match the tune, and a 128K program plays on chip 0 just as its composer mixed it.

## How Klive does it

In Klive, the three chips are one module of the Next core, a C program compiled to WebAssembly. It keeps the
selected chip and the three pans just as the hardware does, ignores a chip select while TurboSound is off, and
sends register selects and data to the selected chip only.

Whenever the mixer needs a sample, Klive spreads each chip's three outputs over the two sides in ABC, ACB or
mono, drops each chip's sides according to its pan, and leaves out a chip that is neither selected nor allowed by
TurboSound. Then it adds the three chips' sides, and hands the result to the mixer as the AY input. How the chips
themselves are stepped is told in the previous chapter's
[How Klive does it](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/psg/#how-klive-does-it). And if
you want to read the code yourself, here is where it lives:

| What | Where |
|---|---|
| The chip select, the pans, TurboSound's switch, ABC, ACB and mono | `src/emu/machines/zxNext/wasm/zxnext/zxnext-psg.c` |
| NextRegs `$06`, `$08` and `$09` | `zxnext-nextreg.c` in the same folder |
| Ports `$FFFD`, `$BFFD` and `$BFF5` | `zxnext-ports.c` in the same folder |

## What to take home

So, what have we got? TurboSound puts three AY chips behind the 128K's ports `$FFFD` and `$BFFD`, and only the
**selected chip** answers. A register select always has bits 7–5 clear, so the Next uses values that look like
`%1 pp 111 cc` to pick a chip instead: `cc` names the chip (`11`, `10` and `01` for chips 0, 1 and 2) and `pp`
sets its pan. Written with both pan bits, they are `$FF`, `$FE` and `$FD`.

Bit 1 of NextReg `$08` switches TurboSound on. While it is off, as after power-on, only the selected chip sounds
and the selection is frozen, which puts every 128K program on chip 0 without it ever knowing.

Each chip spreads its channels in **ABC** (left A + B, right C + B) or **ACB** (left A + C, right C + B), chosen
by bit 5 of NextReg `$08` for all chips, or in **mono** (A + B + C on both sides), chosen per chip by bits 7–5 of
NextReg `$09`. The pan, set again by every select, then lets the chip into the left, the right, both or
neither. Per side, the three chips add at most **1,530** in stereo and **2,295** in mono, comfortably inside the
jack's 2,559 of room above silence.

A reset, or mode `11` in NextReg `$06`, clears the chips, selects chip 0 and puts every pan back in the middle;
a soft reset leaves the NextReg settings alone. 30:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"That’s all. One thing I want you to remember from this little exercise, though: each chip keeps its own\nselected register, like three clerks who each keep their own place in the ledger. So when you switch from one\nchip to another, select the register again, and do not count on the last one you used. The chip itself stays\nselected until your next chip select, which is why a music player usually visits each chip once per frame and\nwrites all of its registers in one go."}]
31:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Reading works the same way: port ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$FFFD"}]," gives you the selected register of the selected chip. And if your\nprogram has lost track of whom it is talking to (it happens to the best of us), port ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$BFF5"}]," will tell it, as\n",["$","$L20",null,{"href":"/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/psg/#ay-or-ym","className":"x:focus-visible:nextra-focus x:text-primary-600 x:underline x:hover:no-underline x:decoration-from-font x:[text-underline-position:from-font]","children":"AY or YM"}]," in the previous chapter\nexplains. Its bits 7–6 read ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"11"}],", ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"10"}]," or ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"01"}],", the same codes the select uses, and bits 4–0 hold the selected\nregister."]}]

35:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"“Frozen” means exactly what it says: the selection stays wherever it was when the music stopped, like a game of\nmusical chairs. Switch TurboSound on, select chip 2, switch TurboSound off again, and chip 2 stays selected. All\nyour writes go to it, and it is the only chip you hear. Nothing is lost meanwhile. All three chips keep their\nregisters, and when you switch TurboSound back on, the other two come back with whatever they held, as if they\nhad never left the room."}]

38:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"Look at the middle one closely. In ABC that is B; in ACB, C. Simple. And the middle seat is the best one in the house: it is where a\nmusician puts the part that must not lean to either side, usually the bass or the melody."}]

40:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"Now, have you wondered why you would want a choice at all? It comes down to how a tune is mixed. Its composer\nhad one layout in mind, and put the part that belongs in the middle on the middle channel. Play it with the\nother layout, and that part slides off to one side while a side part wanders into the middle. On headphones you\nhear it at once: a balanced tune turns lopsided, like a band whose drummer set up in the car park. Spectrum\nmusic was not all written for the same layout, so the Next offers both. And mono? That one is for the tunes\nwritten for the 128K’s single speaker."}]

63:["$","figcaption",null,{"children":"What happens to a value you write to port $FFFD. With bits 7–5 clear, it selects a register in the selected chip; with bit 7 set and bits 4–2 all 1, it selects a chip and sets its pan, as long as TurboSound is on; anything else is ignored.
